<td style="vertical-align:top; text-align:center;border:0;">
</td></tr></tbody></table></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Gabon</b> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ɡ/: &#39;g&#39; in &#39;guy&#39;">ɡ</span><span title="/ə/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;about&#39;">ə</span><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;b&#39; in &#39;buy&#39;">b</span><span title="/ɒ/: &#39;o&#39; in &#39;body&#39;">ɒ</span><span title="&#39;n&#39; in &#39;nigh&#39;">n</span></span>/</a></span></span> <a href="/wiki/Help:Pronunciation_respelling_key" title="Help:Pronunciation respelling key"><i title="English pronunciation respelling">gə-<span style="font-size:90%">BON</span></i></a>; <small>French pronunciation:&#160;&#8203;</small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="fr-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/French" title="Help:IPA/French">[ɡabɔ̃]</a></span>&#32;<span data-nosnippet="" id="ooui-php-1" class="ext-phonos ext-phonos-PhonosButton noexcerpt ext-phonos-PhonosButton-emptylabel oo-ui-widget oo-ui-widget-enabled oo-ui-buttonElement oo-ui-buttonElement-frameless oo-ui-iconElement oo-ui-buttonWidget" data-ooui="{&quot;_&quot;:&quot;mw.Phonos.PhonosButton&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;\/\/upload.wikimedia.org\/wikipedia\/commons\/transcoded\/b\/ba\/Fr-Gabon.oga\/Fr-Gabon.oga.mp3&quot;,&quot;rel&quot;:[&quot;nofollow&quot;],&quot;framed&quot;:false,&quot;icon&quot;:&quot;volumeUp&quot;,&quot;data&quot;:{&quot;ipa&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;text&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;lang&quot;:&quot;en&quot;,&quot;wikibase&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;file&quot;:&quot;Fr-Gabon.oga&quot;},&quot;classes&quot;:[&quot;ext-phonos&quot;,&quot;ext-phonos-PhonosButton&quot;,&quot;noexcerpt&quot;,&quot;ext-phonos-PhonosButton-emptylabel&quot;]}"><a role="button" tabindex="0" href="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/b/ba/Fr-Gabon.oga/Fr-Gabon.oga.mp3" rel="nofollow" aria-label="Play audio" class="oo-ui-buttonElement-button"><span class="oo-ui-iconElement-icon oo-ui-icon-volumeUp"></span><span class="oo-ui-labelElement-label"></span><span class="oo-ui-indicatorElement-indicator oo-ui-indicatorElement-noIndicator"></span></a></span><sup class="ext-phonos-attribution noexcerpt"><a href="/wiki/File:Fr-Gabon.oga" title="File:Fr-Gabon.oga">i</a></sup>; <a href="/wiki/Sangu_language_(Gabon)" title="Sangu language (Gabon)">Sangu</a>: <i lang="snq">Ngabu</i>), officially the <b>Gabonese Republic</b> (<a href="/wiki/French_language" title="French language">French</a>: <i lang="fr">République gabonaise</i>), is a country on the Atlantic coast of West <a href="/wiki/Central_Africa" title="Central Africa">Central Africa</a>. Located on the <a href="/wiki/Equator" title="Equator">equator</a>, it is bordered by <a href="/wiki/Equatorial_Guinea" title="Equatorial Guinea">Equatorial Guinea</a> to the northwest, <a href="/wiki/Cameroon" title="Cameroon">Cameroon</a> to the north, the <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_the_Congo" title="Republic of the Congo">Republic of the Congo</a> on the east and south, and the <a href="/wiki/Gulf_of_Guinea" title="Gulf of Guinea">Gulf of Guinea</a> to the west. It has an area of nearly 270,000 square kilometres (100,000&#160;sq&#160;mi) and its population is estimated at 2.3 million people. There are coastal plains, mountains (the <a href="/wiki/Crystal_Mountains_(Africa)" title="Crystal Mountains (Africa)">Cristal Mountains</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Chaillu_Massif" title="Chaillu Massif">Chaillu Massif</a> in the centre), and a <a href="/wiki/Savanna" title="Savanna">savanna</a> in the east. <a href="/wiki/Libreville" title="Libreville">Libreville</a> is the country's capital and the largest city.
</p><p>Gabon's original inhabitants are the <a href="/wiki/African_Pygmies" title="African Pygmies">pigmy peoples</a>. Beginning in the 14th century, <a href="/wiki/Bantu_expansion" title="Bantu expansion">Bantu migrants</a> began settling in the area as well. Since its independence from France in August 1960, the <a href="/wiki/Sovereign_state" title="Sovereign state">sovereign state</a> of Gabon has had three <a href="/wiki/President_of_Gabon" title="President of Gabon">presidents</a>. In the 1990s, it introduced a multi-party system and a democratic constitution that aimed for a more transparent electoral process and reformed some governmental institutions. Despite this, the <a href="/wiki/Gabonese_Democratic_Party" title="Gabonese Democratic Party">Gabonese Democratic Party</a> (PDG) remains the dominant party. 
</p><p>Gabon is one of the richest countries in Africa in terms of <a href="/wiki/Per_capita_income" title="Per capita income">per capita income</a>, yet large parts of the population are very poor. Omar Bongo of PDG came into office in 1967 and created a kind of "Bongo dynasty", which stabilized its and his small elite power through a so-called clientelism network <a href="/wiki/Fran%C3%A7afrique" title="Françafrique">Françafrique</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Official_language" title="Official language">official language</a> is <a href="/wiki/French_language" title="French language">French</a> and Bantu ethnic groups constitute around 95% of the country's population. <a href="/wiki/Christianity" title="Christianity">Christianity</a> is the nation's predominant religion, practiced by about 76% of the population. With petroleum and foreign private investment, it has the <a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_by_Human_Development_Index" title="List of countries by Human Development Index">fourth highest</a> <a href="/wiki/Human_Development_Index" title="Human Development Index">HDI</a><sup id="cite_ref-HDI_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-HDI-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> in the region (after <a href="/wiki/Mauritius" title="Mauritius">Mauritius</a>, <a href="/wiki/Seychelles" title="Seychelles">Seychelles</a> and <a href="/wiki/South_Africa" title="South Africa">South Africa</a>) and the <a href="/wiki/List_of_African_countries_by_GDP_(PPP)_per_capita" title="List of African countries by GDP (PPP) per capita">fifth highest</a> GDP per capita (PPP) in all of Africa (after Seychelles, Mauritius, <a href="/wiki/Equatorial_Guinea" title="Equatorial Guinea">Equatorial Guinea</a> and <a href="/wiki/Botswana" title="Botswana">Botswana</a>).  
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r886046785">.mw-parser-output .toclimit-2 .toclevel-1 ul,.mw-parser-output .toclimit-3 .toclevel-2 ul,.mw-parser-output .toclimit-4 .toclevel-3 ul,.mw-parser-output .toclimit-5 .toclevel-4 ul,.mw-parser-output .toclimit-6 .toclevel-5 ul,.mw-parser-output .toclimit-7 .toclevel-6 ul{display:none}</style><div class="toclimit-3"><meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" /></div>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="History">History</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabon&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: History">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/History_of_Gabon" title="History of Gabon">History of Gabon</a> and <a href="/wiki/French_Equatorial_Africa" title="French Equatorial Africa">French Equatorial Africa</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Pre-colonization">Pre-colonization</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabon&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Pre-colonization">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p><a href="/wiki/African_Pygmies" title="African Pygmies">Pygmy peoples</a> in the area were largely replaced and absorbed by <a href="/wiki/Bantu_peoples" title="Bantu peoples">Bantu</a> tribes as they migrated. By the 18th century, a <a href="/wiki/Myene_language" title="Myene language">Myeni</a>-speaking kingdom known as the <a href="/wiki/Orungu_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Orungu Kingdom">Kingdom of Orungu</a> formed as a trading centre with the ability to purchase and sell slaves, and fell with the demise of the slave trade in the 1870s.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="French_rule_and_independence">French rule and independence</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabon&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: French rule and independence">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Explorer <a href="/wiki/Pierre_Savorgnan_de_Brazza" title="Pierre Savorgnan de Brazza">Pierre Savorgnan de Brazza</a> led his first mission to the Gabon-Congo area in 1875.<sup id="cite_ref-EB1911_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EB1911-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> He founded the town of <a href="/wiki/Franceville" title="Franceville">Franceville</a> and was later colonial governor. Some Bantu groups lived in the area when France officially occupied it in 1885.
</p><p>In 1910, Gabon became a territory of <a href="/wiki/French_Equatorial_Africa" title="French Equatorial Africa">French Equatorial Africa</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> a federation that survived until 1958. In <a href="/wiki/World_War_II" title="World War II">World War II</a>, the Allies <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Gabon" title="Battle of Gabon">invaded</a> Gabon in order to overthrow the pro-<a href="/wiki/Vichy_France" title="Vichy France">Vichy France</a> colonial administration. On 28 November 1958, Gabon became an autonomous republic within the <a href="/wiki/French_Community" title="French Community">French Community</a>, and on 17 August 1960, it became fully independent.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Independence">Independence</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabon&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Independence">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<h5><span id="M.27ba_rule"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="M'ba_rule">M'ba rule</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabon&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: M&#039;ba rule">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h5>
<p>The first president of Gabon, elected in 1961, was <a href="/wiki/L%C3%A9on_M%27ba" title="Léon M&#39;ba">Léon M'ba</a>, with <a href="/wiki/Omar_Bongo_Ondimba" class="mw-redirect" title="Omar Bongo Ondimba">Omar Bongo Ondimba</a> as his vice president. After M'ba's accession to power, the press was suppressed, political demonstrations suppressed, <a href="/wiki/Freedom_of_expression" class="mw-redirect" title="Freedom of expression">freedom of expression</a> curtailed, other political parties gradually excluded from power, and the Constitution changed along French lines to vest power in the Presidency, a post that M'ba assumed himself. When M'ba dissolved the <a href="/wiki/National_Assembly_(Gabon)" class="mw-redirect" title="National Assembly (Gabon)">National Assembly</a> in January 1964 to institute one-party rule, <a href="/wiki/1964_Gabonese_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat" title="1964 Gabonese coup d&#39;état">an army coup sought to oust him from power</a> and restore parliamentary democracy. French paratroopers flew in within 24 hours to restore M'ba to power. After days of fighting, the coup ended and the opposition was imprisoned, with protests and riots.
</p>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="Bongo_rule_and_PDG">Bongo rule and PDG</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabon&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Bongo rule and PDG">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h5>
<p>When M'Ba died in 1967, Bongo replaced him as president. In March 1968, Bongo declared Gabon a 1-party state by dissolving BDG and establishing a new party – the <a href="/wiki/Parti_D%C3%A9mocratique_Gabonais" class="mw-redirect" title="Parti Démocratique Gabonais">Parti Démocratique Gabonais</a> (PDG). He invited all Gabonese, regardless of previous political affiliation, to participate. Bongo sought to forge a single national movement in support of the government's development policies, using PDG as a tool to submerge the regional and tribal rivalries that had divided Gabonese politics in the past. Bongo was elected president in February 1975; in April 1975, the position of vice president was abolished and replaced by the position of prime minister, who had no right to automatic succession. Bongo was re-elected President in December 1979 and November 1986 to 7-year terms.<sup id="cite_ref-bn_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bn-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:1670_Ogilby_Map_of_West_Africa_(_Gold_Coast,_Slave_Coast,_Ivory_Coast_)_-_Geographicus_-_Guinea-ogilby-1670.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b1/1670_Ogilby_Map_of_West_Africa_%28_Gold_Coast%2C_Slave_Coast%2C_Ivory_Coast_%29_-_Geographicus_-_Guinea-ogilby-1670.jpg/280px-1670_Ogilby_Map_of_West_Africa_%28_Gold_Coast%2C_Slave_Coast%2C_Ivory_Coast_%29_-_Geographicus_-_Guinea-ogilby-1670.jpg" decoding="async" width="280" height="207" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b1/1670_Ogilby_Map_of_West_Africa_%28_Gold_Coast%2C_Slave_Coast%2C_Ivory_Coast_%29_-_Geographicus_-_Guinea-ogilby-1670.jpg/420px-1670_Ogilby_Map_of_West_Africa_%28_Gold_Coast%2C_Slave_Coast%2C_Ivory_Coast_%29_-_Geographicus_-_Guinea-ogilby-1670.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b1/1670_Ogilby_Map_of_West_Africa_%28_Gold_Coast%2C_Slave_Coast%2C_Ivory_Coast_%29_-_Geographicus_-_Guinea-ogilby-1670.jpg/560px-1670_Ogilby_Map_of_West_Africa_%28_Gold_Coast%2C_Slave_Coast%2C_Ivory_Coast_%29_-_Geographicus_-_Guinea-ogilby-1670.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3500" data-file-height="2591" /></a><figcaption>A map of <a href="/wiki/West_Africa" title="West Africa">West Africa</a> in 1670</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Gabon_campaign_%271e_Compagnie_de_Chars_de_Combat_de_la_France_Libre%27.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/Gabon_campaign_%271e_Compagnie_de_Chars_de_Combat_de_la_France_Libre%27.jpg/280px-Gabon_campaign_%271e_Compagnie_de_Chars_de_Combat_de_la_France_Libre%27.jpg" decoding="async" width="280" height="223" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/Gabon_campaign_%271e_Compagnie_de_Chars_de_Combat_de_la_France_Libre%27.jpg/420px-Gabon_campaign_%271e_Compagnie_de_Chars_de_Combat_de_la_France_Libre%27.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/Gabon_campaign_%271e_Compagnie_de_Chars_de_Combat_de_la_France_Libre%27.jpg/560px-Gabon_campaign_%271e_Compagnie_de_Chars_de_Combat_de_la_France_Libre%27.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1531" data-file-height="1220" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Gabon" title="Battle of Gabon">Battle of Gabon</a> resulted in the <a href="/wiki/Free_French_Forces" class="mw-redirect" title="Free French Forces">Free French Forces</a> taking the colony of Gabon from <a href="/wiki/Vichy_France" title="Vichy France">Vichy French</a> forces, 1940</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1990, economic discontent and a desire for political liberalization provoked demonstrations and strikes by students and workers. In response to grievances by workers, Bongo negotiated with them on a sector-by-sector basis, making wage concessions. He promised to open up PDG and to organize a national political conference in March–April 1990 to discuss Gabon's future political system. PDG and 74 political organizations attended the conference. Participants essentially divided into 2 "loose" coalitions, ruling PDG and its allies, and the United Front of Opposition Associations and Parties, consisting of the breakaway Morena Fundamental and the <a href="/wiki/Gabonese_Progress_Party" title="Gabonese Progress Party">Gabonese Progress Party</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-bn_12-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bn-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="Transitional_government_and_RSDG">Transitional government and RSDG</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabon&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Transitional government and RSDG">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h5>
<p>The April 1990 conference approved political reforms, including creation of a national <a href="/wiki/Senate_(Gabon)" class="mw-redirect" title="Senate (Gabon)">Senate</a>, decentralization of the budgetary process, freedom of assembly and press, and cancellation of an <a href="/wiki/Exit_visa" class="mw-redirect" title="Exit visa">exit visa</a> requirement. In an attempt to guide the political system's transformation to multiparty democracy, Bongo resigned as PDG chairman and created a transitional government headed by a new Prime Minister, <a href="/wiki/Casimir_Oye-Mba" class="mw-redirect" title="Casimir Oye-Mba">Casimir Oye-Mba</a>. The Gabonese Social Democratic Grouping (RSDG), as the resulting government was called, was smaller than the previous government and included representatives from some opposition parties in its cabinet. RSDG drafted a provisional constitution in May 1990 that provided a basic <a href="/wiki/Bill_of_rights" title="Bill of rights">bill of rights</a> and an independent judiciary and retained "strong" executive powers for the president. After further review by a constitutional committee and the National Assembly, this document came into force in March 1991.<sup id="cite_ref-bn_12-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bn-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Opposition to PDG continued after the April 1990 conference, and in September 1990, two <a href="/wiki/Coup_d%27%C3%A9tat" title="Coup d&#39;état">coup d'état</a> attempts were uncovered and aborted. With demonstrations after the death of an opposition leader, the first multiparty National Assembly elections in almost 30 years took place in September–October 1990, with PDG garnering a majority.<sup id="cite_ref-bn_12-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bn-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h5><span id="Bongo.27s_re-election_and_rule"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Bongo's_re-election_and_rule">Bongo's re-election and rule</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabon&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Bongo&#039;s re-election and rule">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h5>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Omar_Bongo_with_George_Bush_May_26_2004-01.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b8/Omar_Bongo_with_George_Bush_May_26_2004-01.jpg/280px-Omar_Bongo_with_George_Bush_May_26_2004-01.jpg" decoding="async" width="280" height="193" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b8/Omar_Bongo_with_George_Bush_May_26_2004-01.jpg/420px-Omar_Bongo_with_George_Bush_May_26_2004-01.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/b/b8/Omar_Bongo_with_George_Bush_May_26_2004-01.jpg 2x" data-file-width="514" data-file-height="355" /></a><figcaption>President <a href="/wiki/George_W._Bush" title="George W. Bush">George W. Bush</a> welcomes President <a href="/wiki/Omar_Bongo" title="Omar Bongo">Omar Bongo</a> to the <a href="/wiki/Oval_Office" title="Oval Office">Oval Office</a>, May 2004</figcaption></figure>
<p>Following President <a href="/wiki/Omar_Bongo" title="Omar Bongo">Omar Bongo</a>'s re-election in December 1993 with 51% of the vote, opposition candidates refused to validate the election results. Civil disturbances and violent repression led to an agreement between the government and opposition factions to work toward a political settlement. These talks led to the Paris Accords in November 1994, under which some opposition figures were included in a government of national unity. This arrangement broke down and the 1996 and 1997 legislative and municipal elections provided the background for renewed partisan politics. PDG won in the legislative election, and some cities, including <a href="/wiki/Libreville" title="Libreville">Libreville</a>, elected opposition mayors during the 1997 local election.<sup id="cite_ref-bn_12-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bn-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h6><span class="mw-headline" id="Boycott_of_elections_and_crisis">Boycott of elections and crisis</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabon&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Boycott of elections and crisis">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h6>
<p>Facing a divided opposition, President Omar Bongo coasted to re-election in December 1998. While some of Bongo's opponents rejected the outcome as fraudulent, some international observers characterized the results as representative "despite many perceived irregularities". Legislative elections held in 2001–2002 were boycotted by a number of smaller opposition parties and were criticized for their administrative weaknesses, produced a National Assembly dominated by PDG and allied independents. In November 2005 President Omar Bongo was elected for his sixth term. He won re-election, and opponents claim that the balloting process was marred by irregularities. There were some instances of violence following the announcement of his win.<sup id="cite_ref-bn_12-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bn-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> National Assembly elections were held in December 2006. Some seats contested because of voting irregularities were overturned by the <a href="/wiki/Constitutional_Court_of_Gabon" title="Constitutional Court of Gabon">Constitutional Court</a>, and the subsequent run-off elections in 2007 yielded a PDG-controlled National Assembly.<sup id="cite_ref-bn_12-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bn-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="Death_of_Bongo_and_succession">Death of Bongo and succession</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabon&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Death of Bongo and succession">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h5>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Independence_Celebration_in_Gabon.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/Independence_Celebration_in_Gabon.JPG/280px-Independence_Celebration_in_Gabon.JPG" decoding="async" width="280" height="210" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/Independence_Celebration_in_Gabon.JPG/420px-Independence_Celebration_in_Gabon.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/Independence_Celebration_in_Gabon.JPG/560px-Independence_Celebration_in_Gabon.JPG 2x" data-file-width="4320" data-file-height="3240" /></a><figcaption>Independence Day celebration in Gabon</figcaption></figure>
<p>On 8 June 2009, President Omar Bongo died of cardiac arrest at a Spanish hospital in Barcelona. In accordance with the amended constitution, <a href="/wiki/Rose_Francine_Rogomb%C3%A9" title="Rose Francine Rogombé">Rose Francine Rogombé</a>, the President of the Senate, became Interim President on 10 June 2009. The first contested elections in Gabon's history that did not include Omar Bongo as a candidate were held on 30 August 2009, with 18 candidates for president. The lead-up to the elections saw some isolated protests. Omar Bongo's son, ruling party leader <a href="/wiki/Ali_Bongo_Ondimba" class="mw-redirect" title="Ali Bongo Ondimba">Ali Bongo</a>, was formally declared the winner after a 3-week review by the Constitutional Court; his inauguration took place on 16 October 2009.<sup id="cite_ref-bn_12-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bn-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> The court's review had been prompted by claims of fraud by some opposition candidates, with the initial announcement of election results sparking violent protests in <a href="/wiki/Port-Gentil" title="Port-Gentil">Port-Gentil</a>. The citizens of Port-Gentil took to the streets, and some shops and residences were burned, including the French Consulate and a local prison. Officially, 4 deaths occurred during the riots. Gendarmes and the military were deployed to Port-Gentil to support the beleaguered police, and a curfew was in effect for more than 3 months.<sup id="cite_ref-bn_12-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bn-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A partial legislative by-election was held in June 2010. A coalition of parties, the Union Nationale (UN), participated for the first time. UN is composed mostly of PDG defectors who left the party after Omar Bongo's death. Of the 5 contested seats, PDG won 3 and UN won 2; both sides claimed victory.<sup id="cite_ref-bn_12-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bn-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In January 2019, there was an <a href="/wiki/2019_Gabonese_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat_attempt" class="mw-redirect" title="2019 Gabonese coup d&#39;état attempt">attempted coup d'état</a> led by soldiers against the President Ali Bongo; the coup ultimately failed.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In June 2021, Gabon became the first country to receive payments for reduced emissions from <a href="/wiki/Deforestation" title="Deforestation">deforestation</a> and <a href="/wiki/Forest_degradation" title="Forest degradation">forest degradation</a>. 
</p><p>In June 2022, Gabon and <a href="/wiki/Togo" title="Togo">Togo</a> joined the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations" title="Commonwealth of Nations">Commonwealth of Nations</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span id="2023_coup_d.27.C3.A9tat"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="2023_coup_d'état">2023 coup d'état</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabon&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: 2023 coup d&#039;état">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/2023_Gabonese_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat" title="2023 Gabonese coup d&#39;état">2023 Gabonese coup d'état</a></div>
<p>In August 2023, following the announcement that Ali Bongo had won a third term in the <a href="/wiki/2023_Gabonese_general_election" title="2023 Gabonese general election">general election</a>, military officers announced that they had taken power in a <a href="/wiki/2023_Gabonese_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat" title="2023 Gabonese coup d&#39;état">coup d'état</a> and cancelled the election results. They also dissolved state institutions including the Judiciary, Parliament and the constitutional assembly.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>  On 31 August 2023, army officers who seized power, ending the Bongo family's 55-year hold on power, named Gen <a href="/wiki/Brice_Clotaire_Oligui_Nguema" title="Brice Clotaire Oligui Nguema">Brice Oligui Nguema</a> as the country's transitional leader.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Politics">Politics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabon&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Politics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Politics_of_Gabon" title="Politics of Gabon">Politics of Gabon</a></div>
<p>The presidential republic form of government is stated under the 1961 constitution (revised in 1975, rewritten in 1991, and revised in 2003). The president is elected by universal suffrage for a seven-year term; a 2003 constitutional amendment removed presidential term limits. The president can appoint and dismiss the prime minister, the cabinet, and judges of the independent Supreme Court. The president has other powers such as authority to dissolve the National Assembly, declare a state of siege, delay legislation, and conduct referendums.<sup id="cite_ref-bn_12-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bn-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> Gabon has a <a href="/wiki/Parliament_of_Gabon" title="Parliament of Gabon">bicameral legislature</a> with a National Assembly and Senate. The National Assembly has 120 deputies who are popularly elected for a five-year term. The Senate is composed of 102 members who are elected by municipal councils and regional assemblies and serve for six years. The Senate was created in the 1990–1991 constitutional revision, and was not brought into being until after the 1997 local elections. The President of the Senate is next in succession to the President.<sup id="cite_ref-bn_12-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bn-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1990, the government made changes to Gabon's political system. A transitional constitution was drafted in May 1990 as an outgrowth of the national political conference in March–April and later revised by a constitutional committee. Among its provisions were a Western-style <a href="/wiki/Bill_of_rights" title="Bill of rights">bill of rights</a>, creation of a National Council of Democracy to oversee the guarantee of those rights, a governmental advisory board on economic and social issues, and an independent judiciary. After approval by the National Assembly, PDG Central Committee, and the President, the Assembly unanimously adopted the constitution in March 1991. Multiparty legislative elections were held in 1990–1991 when opposition parties had not been declared formally legal. In January 1991, the Assembly passed by unanimous vote a law governing the legalization of opposition parties.<sup id="cite_ref-bn_12-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bn-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After President <a href="/wiki/Omar_Bongo" title="Omar Bongo">Omar Bongo</a> was re-elected in 1993, in a disputed election where only 51% of votes were cast, social and political disturbances led to the 1994 Paris Conference and Accords. These provided a framework for the next elections. Local and legislative elections were delayed until 1996–1997. In 1997, constitutional amendments put forward years earlier were adopted to create the Senate and the position of Vice President, and to extend the President's term to seven years.<sup id="cite_ref-bn_12-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bn-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In October 2009, President <a href="/wiki/Ali_Bongo_Ondimba" class="mw-redirect" title="Ali Bongo Ondimba">Ali Bongo Ondimba</a> began efforts to streamline the government. In an effort to reduce corruption and government bloat, he eliminated 17 minister-level positions, abolished the Vice Presidency and reorganized the portfolios of some ministries, bureaus and directorates. In November 2009, President Bongo Ondimba announced a new vision for the modernization of Gabon, called "Gabon Emergent". This program contains three pillars: Green Gabon, Service Gabon, and Industrial Gabon. The goals of Gabon Emergent are to diversify the economy so that Gabon becomes less reliant on petroleum, to eliminate corruption, and to modernize the workforce. Under this program, exports of raw timber have been banned, a government-wide census was held, the work day was changed to eliminate a long midday break, and a national oil company was created.<sup id="cite_ref-bn_12-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bn-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 25 January 2011, opposition leader <a href="/wiki/Andr%C3%A9_Mba_Obame" title="André Mba Obame">André Mba Obame</a> claimed the presidency, saying the country should be run by someone the people really wanted. He selected 19 ministers for his government, and the entire group, along with hundreds of others, spent the night at the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations" title="United Nations">United Nations</a> headquarters. On January 26, the government dissolved Mba Obame's party. AU chairman <a href="/wiki/Jean_Ping" title="Jean Ping">Jean Ping</a> said that Mba Obame's action "hurts the integrity of legitimate institutions and also endangers the peace, the security and the stability of Gabon."<sup id="cite_ref-Goma_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Goma-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> Interior Minister Jean-François Ndongou accused Mba Obame and his supporters of <a href="/wiki/Treason" title="Treason">treason</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Goma_18-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Goma-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/UN_Secretary-General" class="mw-redirect" title="UN Secretary-General">UN Secretary-General</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ban_Ki-moon" title="Ban Ki-moon">Ban Ki-moon</a>, said that he recognized Ondimba as the only official Gabonese president.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability#Self-published_sources" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability"><span title="The material near this tag may rely on a self-published source. (February 2020)">self-published source?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Gabonese_presidential_election,_2016" class="mw-redirect" title="Gabonese presidential election, 2016">2016 presidential election</a> was disputed, with "very close" official results reported. Protests broke out in the capital and met a repression which culminated in the alleged bombing of opposition party headquarters by the presidential guard. Between 50 and 100 citizens were killed by security forces and 1,000 arrested.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> International observers criticized irregularities, including unnaturally high turnout reported for some districts. The country's supreme court threw out some suspect precincts, and the ballots have been destroyed. The election was declared in favor of the incumbent Ondimba. The European Parliament issued two resolutions denouncing the unclear results of the election and calling for an investigation on the human rights violations.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A few days after the controversial <a href="/wiki/2023_Gabonese_general_election" title="2023 Gabonese general election">presidential election</a> in August 2023, a group of military officials declared a <a href="/wiki/2023_Gabonese_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat" title="2023 Gabonese coup d&#39;état">military coup</a> and that they had overthrown the government and deposed Ali Bongo Ondimba. The announcement came hours after <a href="/wiki/Ali_Bongo_Ondimba" class="mw-redirect" title="Ali Bongo Ondimba">Ali Bongo</a> was officially re-elected for a third term.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Brice_Clotaire_Oligui_Nguema" title="Brice Clotaire Oligui Nguema">General Brice Oligui Nguema</a> was appointed as the transitional leader. This event marked the eighth instance of military intervention in the region since 2020, raising concerns about democratic stability.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_relations">Foreign relations</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabon&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Foreign relations">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Gabon" title="Foreign relations of Gabon">Foreign relations of Gabon</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Vladimir_Putin_with_Julien_Nkoghe_Bekale_%26_his_spouse.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Vladimir_Putin_with_Julien_Nkoghe_Bekale_%26_his_spouse.jpg/280px-Vladimir_Putin_with_Julien_Nkoghe_Bekale_%26_his_spouse.jpg" decoding="async" width="280" height="173" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Vladimir_Putin_with_Julien_Nkoghe_Bekale_%26_his_spouse.jpg/420px-Vladimir_Putin_with_Julien_Nkoghe_Bekale_%26_his_spouse.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Vladimir_Putin_with_Julien_Nkoghe_Bekale_%26_his_spouse.jpg/560px-Vladimir_Putin_with_Julien_Nkoghe_Bekale_%26_his_spouse.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1880" data-file-height="1160" /></a><figcaption>Prime Minister of Gabon <a href="/wiki/Julien_Nkoghe_Bekale" title="Julien Nkoghe Bekale">Julien Nkoghe Bekale</a> and Russian President <a href="/wiki/Vladimir_Putin" title="Vladimir Putin">Vladimir Putin</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Russia-Africa_Summit" class="mw-redirect" title="Russia-Africa Summit">Russia-Africa Summit</a> in <a href="/wiki/Sochi" title="Sochi">Sochi</a> in Russia in October 2019</figcaption></figure>
<p>Since independence, Gabon has followed a nonaligned policy, advocating dialogue in international affairs and recognizing each side of divided countries. In intra-African affairs, it espouses development by evolution rather than revolution and favors regulated private enterprise as the system most likely to promote rapid economic growth. It involved itself in mediation efforts in <a href="/wiki/Chad" title="Chad">Chad</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Central_African_Republic" title="Central African Republic">Central African Republic</a>, <a href="/wiki/Angola" title="Angola">Angola</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_the_Congo" title="Republic of the Congo">Republic of the Congo</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Democratic_Republic_of_the_Congo" title="Democratic Republic of the Congo">Democratic Republic of the Congo</a> (D.R.C.), and <a href="/wiki/Burundi" title="Burundi">Burundi</a>. In December 1999, through the mediation efforts of President Bongo, a peace accord was signed in the Republic of the Congo (Brazzaville) between the government and most leaders of an armed rebellion. President Bongo was involved in the continuing D.R.C. peace process, and played a role in mediating the crisis in <a href="/wiki/Ivory_Coast" title="Ivory Coast">Ivory Coast</a>.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:US_Navy_080107-N-4044H-049_Capt._John_B._Nowell_Jr.,_commodore,_Africa_Partnership_Station_(APS)_is_greeted_by_Gabonese_Army_as_he_arrives_at_a_scheduled_meeting_with_General_Ntori_Longo,_chief_of_Gabonese_Armed_Services,_about.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/US_Navy_080107-N-4044H-049_Capt._John_B._Nowell_Jr.%2C_commodore%2C_Africa_Partnership_Station_%28APS%29_is_greeted_by_Gabonese_Army_as_he_arrives_at_a_scheduled_meeting_with_General_Ntori_Longo%2C_chief_of_Gabonese_Armed_Services%2C_about.jpg/280px-thumbnail.jpg" decoding="async" width="280" height="200" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/US_Navy_080107-N-4044H-049_Capt._John_B._Nowell_Jr.%2C_commodore%2C_Africa_Partnership_Station_%28APS%29_is_greeted_by_Gabonese_Army_as_he_arrives_at_a_scheduled_meeting_with_General_Ntori_Longo%2C_chief_of_Gabonese_Armed_Services%2C_about.jpg/420px-thumbnail.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/US_Navy_080107-N-4044H-049_Capt._John_B._Nowell_Jr.%2C_commodore%2C_Africa_Partnership_Station_%28APS%29_is_greeted_by_Gabonese_Army_as_he_arrives_at_a_scheduled_meeting_with_General_Ntori_Longo%2C_chief_of_Gabonese_Armed_Services%2C_about.jpg/560px-thumbnail.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2520" data-file-height="1800" /></a><figcaption>A U.S. Navy Captain greeted by the Gabonese Army</figcaption></figure>
<p>Gabon is a member of the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations" title="United Nations">United Nations</a> (UN) and some of its specialized and related agencies, and of the <a href="/wiki/World_Bank" title="World Bank">World Bank</a>; the <a href="/wiki/IMF" class="mw-redirect" title="IMF">IMF</a>; the <a href="/wiki/African_Union" title="African Union">African Union</a> (AU); the Central African Customs Union/Central African Economic and Monetary Community (UDEAC/CEMAC); EU/ACP association under the <a href="/wiki/Lom%C3%A9_Convention" title="Lomé Convention">Lomé Convention</a>; the <a href="/wiki/Communaute_Financiere_Africaine" class="mw-redirect" title="Communaute Financiere Africaine">Communaute Financiere Africaine</a> (CFA); the <a href="/wiki/Organization_of_the_Islamic_Conference" class="mw-redirect" title="Organization of the Islamic Conference">Organization of the Islamic Conference</a> (OIC); the <a href="/wiki/Nonaligned_Movement" class="mw-redirect" title="Nonaligned Movement">Nonaligned Movement</a>; and the <a href="/wiki/Economic_Community_of_Central_African_States" title="Economic Community of Central African States">Economic Community of Central African States</a> (ECCAS/CEEAC). In 1995, Gabon withdrew from the <a href="/wiki/Organization_of_the_Petroleum_Exporting_Countries" class="mw-redirect" title="Organization of the Petroleum Exporting Countries">Organization of the Petroleum Exporting Countries</a> (OPEC), rejoining in 2016. Gabon was elected to a non-permanent seat on the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council" title="United Nations Security Council">United Nations Security Council</a> for January 2010 through December 2011 and held the rotating presidency in March 2010.<sup id="cite_ref-bn_12-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bn-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> In 2022, Gabon joined the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations" title="Commonwealth of Nations">Commonwealth of Nations</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Military">Military</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabon&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Military">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Armed_Forces_of_Gabon" title="Armed Forces of Gabon">Armed Forces of Gabon</a></div>
<p>It has a professional military of about 5,000 personnel, divided into <a href="/wiki/Gabonese_Army" class="mw-redirect" title="Gabonese Army">army</a>, <a href="/wiki/Gabonese_Navy" title="Gabonese Navy">navy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Gabonese_Air_Force" title="Gabonese Air Force">air force</a>, <a href="/wiki/Gendarmerie" title="Gendarmerie">gendarmerie</a>, and police force.  A 1,800-member guard provides security for the president.<sup id="cite_ref-bn_12-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bn-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Administrative_divisions">Administrative divisions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabon&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Administrative divisions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-halign-right noresize mw-ext-imagemap-desc-bottom-left" typeof="mw:File"><span title="A clickable map of Gabon exhibiting its nine provinces."><img alt="A clickable map of Gabon exhibiting its nine provinces." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/c/cf/Gabon_provinces_numbered.png" decoding="async" width="300" height="320" class="mw-file-element" data-file-width="300" data-file-height="320" usemap="#ImageMap_6af12da147022f1e" resource="/wiki/File:Gabon_provinces_numbered.png" /></span><map name="ImageMap_6af12da147022f1e"><area href="/wiki/Estuaire_Province" shape="rect" coords="53,82,92,115" alt="Estuaire Province" title="Estuaire Province" /><area href="/wiki/Haut-Ogoou%C3%A9" shape="rect" coords="237,161,274,204" alt="Haut-Ogooué" title="Haut-Ogooué" /><area href="/wiki/Moyen-Ogoou%C3%A9" shape="rect" coords="88,118,116,151" alt="Moyen-Ogooué" title="Moyen-Ogooué" /><area href="/wiki/Ngouni%C3%A9" shape="rect" coords="105,176,144,218" alt="Ngounié" title="Ngounié" /><area href="/wiki/Nyanga_Province" shape="rect" coords="109,248,136,284" alt="Nyanga Province" title="Nyanga Province" /><area href="/wiki/Ogoou%C3%A9-Ivindo" shape="rect" coords="192,65,218,99" alt="Ogooué-Ivindo" title="Ogooué-Ivindo" /><area href="/wiki/Ogoou%C3%A9-Lolo" shape="rect" coords="176,147,212,188" alt="Ogooué-Lolo" title="Ogooué-Lolo" /><area href="/wiki/Ogoou%C3%A9-Maritime" shape="rect" coords="29,167,67,218" alt="Ogooué-Maritime" title="Ogooué-Maritime" /><area href="/wiki/Woleu-Ntem" shape="rect" coords="146,20,183,63" alt="Woleu-Ntem" title="Woleu-Ntem" /></map><figcaption>A clickable map of Gabon exhibiting its nine provinces.</figcaption></figure>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Subdivisions_of_Gabon" title="Subdivisions of Gabon">Subdivisions of Gabon</a></div>
<p>It is divided into 9 provinces which are subdivided into 50 <a href="/wiki/Departments_of_Gabon" title="Departments of Gabon">departments</a>. The president appoints the provincial governors, the prefects, and the subprefects.<sup id="cite_ref-bn_12-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bn-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The provinces are (capitals in parentheses):
</p>
<ol><li><a href="/wiki/Estuaire_Province" title="Estuaire Province">Estuaire</a> (<a href="/wiki/Libreville" title="Libreville">Libreville</a>)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Haut-Ogoou%C3%A9_Province" title="Haut-Ogooué Province">Haut-Ogooué</a> (<a href="/wiki/Franceville" title="Franceville">Franceville</a>)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Moyen-Ogoou%C3%A9_Province" title="Moyen-Ogooué Province">Moyen-Ogooué</a> (<a href="/wiki/Lambar%C3%A9n%C3%A9" title="Lambaréné">Lambaréné</a>)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Ngouni%C3%A9_Province" title="Ngounié Province">Ngounié</a> (<a href="/wiki/Mouila" title="Mouila">Mouila</a>)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Nyanga_Province" title="Nyanga Province">Nyanga</a> (<a href="/wiki/Tchibanga" title="Tchibanga">Tchibanga</a>)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Ogoou%C3%A9-Ivindo_Province" title="Ogooué-Ivindo Province">Ogooué-Ivindo</a> (<a href="/wiki/Makokou" title="Makokou">Makokou</a>)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Ogoou%C3%A9-Lolo_Province" title="Ogooué-Lolo Province">Ogooué-Lolo</a> (<a href="/wiki/Koulamoutou" title="Koulamoutou">Koulamoutou</a>)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Ogoou%C3%A9-Maritime_Province" title="Ogooué-Maritime Province">Ogooué-Maritime</a> (<a href="/wiki/Port-Gentil" title="Port-Gentil">Port-Gentil</a>)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Woleu-Ntem_Province" title="Woleu-Ntem Province">Woleu-Ntem</a> (<a href="/wiki/Oyem" title="Oyem">Oyem</a>)</li></ol>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Geography">Geography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabon&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Geography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Geography_of_Gabon" title="Geography of Gabon">Geography of Gabon</a> and <a href="/wiki/Geology_of_Gabon" title="Geology of Gabon">Geology of Gabon</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Gabon_sat.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f7/Gabon_sat.png/220px-Gabon_sat.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="238" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f7/Gabon_sat.png/330px-Gabon_sat.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f7/Gabon_sat.png/440px-Gabon_sat.png 2x" data-file-width="698" data-file-height="756" /></a><figcaption>Satellite image of Gabon</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Koppen-Geiger_Map_GAB_present.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f6/Koppen-Geiger_Map_GAB_present.svg/220px-Koppen-Geiger_Map_GAB_present.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="163" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f6/Koppen-Geiger_Map_GAB_present.svg/330px-Koppen-Geiger_Map_GAB_present.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f6/Koppen-Geiger_Map_GAB_present.svg/440px-Koppen-Geiger_Map_GAB_present.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1150" data-file-height="850" /></a><figcaption>Map of Köppen climate classification</figcaption></figure>
<p>It is located on the <a href="/wiki/Atlantic_Ocean" title="Atlantic Ocean">Atlantic</a> coast of central Africa on the <a href="/wiki/Equator" title="Equator">equator</a>, between latitudes <a href="/wiki/3rd_parallel_north" title="3rd parallel north">3°N</a> and <a href="/wiki/4th_parallel_south" title="4th parallel south">4°S</a>, and longitudes <a href="/wiki/8th_meridian_east" title="8th meridian east">8°</a> and <a href="/wiki/15th_meridian_east" title="15th meridian east">15°E</a>. Gabon has an <a href="/wiki/Equatorial_climate" class="mw-redirect" title="Equatorial climate">equatorial climate</a> with a system of <a href="/wiki/Tropical_rainforest" title="Tropical rainforest">rainforests</a>, with 89.3% of its land area forested.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>There are coastal plains (ranging between 20 and 300&#160;km&#160;[10 and 190&#160;mi] from the ocean's shore), the mountains (the Cristal Mountains to the northeast of Libreville, the Chaillu Massif in the centre), and the <a href="/wiki/Savanna" title="Savanna">savanna</a> in the east. The coastal plains form a section of the <a href="/wiki/World_Wildlife_Fund" class="mw-redirect" title="World Wildlife Fund">World Wildlife Fund</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Atlantic_Equatorial_coastal_forests" title="Atlantic Equatorial coastal forests">Atlantic Equatorial coastal forests</a> <a href="/wiki/Ecoregion" title="Ecoregion">ecoregion</a> and contain patches of <a href="/wiki/Central_African_mangroves" title="Central African mangroves">Central African mangroves</a> including on the <a href="/wiki/Muni_River" title="Muni River">Muni River</a> estuary on the border with <a href="/wiki/Equatorial_Guinea" title="Equatorial Guinea">Equatorial Guinea</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-DinersteinOlson2017_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DinersteinOlson2017-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Geologically, Gabon is primarily <a href="/wiki/Archean" title="Archean">Archean</a> and <a href="/wiki/Paleoproterozoic" title="Paleoproterozoic">Paleoproterozoic</a> igneous and <a href="/wiki/Metamorphic_basement_rock" class="mw-redirect" title="Metamorphic basement rock">metamorphic basement rock</a>, belonging to the stable continental crust of the <a href="/wiki/Congo_Craton" title="Congo Craton">Congo Craton</a>. Some formations are more than 2 billion years old. Some rock units are overlain by marine <a href="/wiki/Carbonate" title="Carbonate">carbonate</a>, lacustrine and continental sedimentary rocks, and unconsolidated sediments and soils that formed in the last 2.5 million years of the <a href="/wiki/Quaternary" title="Quaternary">Quaternary</a>. The rifting apart of the <a href="/wiki/Supercontinent" title="Supercontinent">supercontinent</a> <a href="/wiki/Pangaea" title="Pangaea">Pangaea</a> created rift basins that filled with sediments and formed the hydrocarbons.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> There are Oklo reactor zones, a <a href="/wiki/Natural_nuclear_fission_reactor" title="Natural nuclear fission reactor">natural nuclear fission reactor</a> on Earth which was active 2 billion years ago. The site was discovered during uranium mining in the 1970s to supply the French nuclear power industry.
</p><p>Its largest river is the <a href="/wiki/Ogoou%C3%A9" class="mw-redirect" title="Ogooué">Ogooué</a> which is 1,200 kilometres (750&#160;mi) long. It has 3 <a href="/wiki/Karst_topography" class="mw-redirect" title="Karst topography">karst</a> areas where there are hundreds of caves located in the dolomite and limestone rocks. A National Geographic Expedition visited some caves in the summer of 2008 to document them.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2002, President Omar Bongo Ondimba designated roughly 10% of the nation's territory to be part of its <a href="/wiki/List_of_national_parks_of_Gabon" title="List of national parks of Gabon">national park system</a> (with 13 parks in total). The <a href="/wiki/National_Agency_for_National_Parks" title="National Agency for National Parks">National Agency for National Parks</a> manages Gabon's national park system. Gabon had a 2018 <a href="/wiki/Forest_Landscape_Integrity_Index" title="Forest Landscape Integrity Index">Forest Landscape Integrity Index</a> mean score of 9.07/10, ranking it 9th globally out of 172 countries.<sup id="cite_ref-FLII-Supplementary_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FLII-Supplementary-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Wildlife">Wildlife</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabon&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Wildlife">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Wildlife_of_Gabon" title="Wildlife of Gabon">Wildlife of Gabon</a></div>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Economy">Economy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabon&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Economy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Economy_of_Gabon" title="Economy of Gabon">Economy of Gabon</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Gabon_Product_Exports_(2019).svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d7/Gabon_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg/290px-Gabon_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg.png" decoding="async" width="290" height="189" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d7/Gabon_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg/435px-Gabon_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d7/Gabon_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg/580px-Gabon_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1100" data-file-height="715" /></a><figcaption>A proportional representation of Gabon exports, 2019</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:GDP_per_capita_development_of_Gabon.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b8/GDP_per_capita_development_of_Gabon.svg/220px-GDP_per_capita_development_of_Gabon.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="155" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b8/GDP_per_capita_development_of_Gabon.svg/330px-GDP_per_capita_development_of_Gabon.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b8/GDP_per_capita_development_of_Gabon.svg/440px-GDP_per_capita_development_of_Gabon.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="850" data-file-height="600" /></a><figcaption>Change in per capita GDP of Gabon, 1950–2018. Figures are inflation-adjusted to 2011 International dollars.</figcaption></figure>
<p><a href="/wiki/Oil_revenue" class="mw-redirect" title="Oil revenue">Oil revenues</a> constitute roughly 46% of the government's budget, 43% of the gross domestic product (GDP), and 81% of exports. Oil production declined from its higher point of 370,000 barrels per day in 1997. Some estimates suggest that Gabonese oil will be expended by 2025. Planning is beginning for an after-oil scenario.<sup id="cite_ref-bn_12-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bn-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> The Grondin Oil Field was discovered in 50&#160;m (160&#160;ft) water depths 40&#160;km (25&#160;mi) offshore, in 1971 and produces from the Batanga <a href="/wiki/Sandstone" title="Sandstone">sandstones</a> of <a href="/wiki/Maastrichtian" title="Maastrichtian">Maastrichtian</a> age forming an <a href="/wiki/Anticline" title="Anticline">anticline</a> salt <a href="/wiki/Structural_trap" class="mw-redirect" title="Structural trap">structural trap</a> which is about 2&#160;km (1.2&#160;mi) deep.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>"Overspending" on the <a href="/wiki/Trans-Gabon_Railway" title="Trans-Gabon Railway">Trans-Gabon Railway</a>, the CFA franc devaluation of 1994, and periods of lower oil prices caused debt problems.<sup id="cite_ref-bn_12-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bn-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Successive <a href="/wiki/International_Monetary_Fund" title="International Monetary Fund">International Monetary Fund</a> (IMF) missions have criticized the Gabonaise government for overspending on off-budget items (in good years and bad), over-borrowing from the <a href="/wiki/Bank_of_Central_African_States" title="Bank of Central African States">central bank</a>, and slipping on the schedule for <a href="/wiki/Privatization" title="Privatization">privatization</a> and administrative reform. In September 2005 Gabon successfully concluded a 15-month Stand-By Arrangement with the IMF. A three-year Stand-By Arrangement with IMF was approved in May 2007. Because of the financial crisis and social developments surrounding the death of President Omar Bongo and the elections, Gabon was unable to meet its economic goals under the Stand-By Arrangement in 2009.<sup id="cite_ref-bn_12-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bn-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gabon's oil revenues have given it a per capita GDP of $8,600. A "skewed income distribution" and "poor social indicators" are "evident".<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> The richest 20% of the population earn over 90% of the income while about a third of the Gabonese population lives in poverty.<sup id="cite_ref-bn_12-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bn-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The economy is dependent on extraction. Before the discovery of oil, logging was the "pillar" of the Gabonese economy. Then, logging and manganese mining are the "next-most-important" income generators. Some explorations suggest the presence of the world's largest unexploited iron ore deposit. For some who live in rural areas without access to employment opportunity in extractive industries, remittances from family members in urban areas or subsistence activities provide income.<sup id="cite_ref-bn_12-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bn-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Foreign and local observers have lamented the lack of diversity in the Gabonese economy. Factors that have "limited the development of new industries" were listed as follows:
</p>
<ul><li>the market is "small", about a million</li>
<li>dependent on imports from France</li>
<li>unable to capitalize on regional markets</li>
<li>entrepreneurial zeal not always present among the Gabonese</li>
<li>a "fairly regular" stream of oil "rent", even if it is diminishing</li></ul>
<p>Further investment in the agricultural or tourism sectors is "complicated by poor infrastructure". Some processing and service sectors are "largely dominated by a few prominent local investors".<sup id="cite_ref-bn_12-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bn-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At World Bank and IMF insistence, the government embarked in the 1990s on a program of privatization of its state-owned companies and administrative reform, including reducing public sector employment and salary growth. A government has voiced a commitment to work toward an economic transformation of the country.<sup id="cite_ref-bn_12-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bn-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Demographics">Demographics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabon&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Demographics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Demographics_of_Gabon" title="Demographics of Gabon">Demographics of Gabon</a></div>

<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Crowd_on_Beach_in_Gabon.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2f/Crowd_on_Beach_in_Gabon.JPG/220px-Crowd_on_Beach_in_Gabon.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2f/Crowd_on_Beach_in_Gabon.JPG/330px-Crowd_on_Beach_in_Gabon.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2f/Crowd_on_Beach_in_Gabon.JPG/440px-Crowd_on_Beach_in_Gabon.JPG 2x" data-file-width="4320" data-file-height="3240" /></a><figcaption>Crowd on beach</figcaption></figure>
<p>It has a population of approximately 2.3 million.<sup id="cite_ref-UN_WPP_32-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-UN_WPP-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-UN_WPP_2022_33-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-UN_WPP_2022-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> Historical and environmental factors caused its population to decline between 1900 and 1940.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> It has one of the lowest <a href="/wiki/Population_density" title="Population density">population densities</a> of any country in Africa,<sup id="cite_ref-bn_12-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bn-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> and the fourth highest <a href="/wiki/Human_Development_Index" title="Human Development Index">Human Development Index</a> in <a href="/wiki/Sub-Saharan_Africa" title="Sub-Saharan Africa">Sub-Saharan Africa</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-HDI_7-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-HDI-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Ethnic_groups">Ethnic groups</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabon&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Ethnic groups">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Gabon has at least 40 <a href="/wiki/Ethnic_group" class="mw-redirect" title="Ethnic group">ethnic groups</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-bn_12-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bn-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> including <a href="/wiki/Fang_people" title="Fang people">Fang</a>, <a href="/wiki/Myene_language" title="Myene language">Myènè</a>, <a href="/wiki/Punu_people" title="Punu people">Punu</a>-Échira, <a href="/wiki/Nzebi_languages" title="Nzebi languages">Nzebi</a>-Adouma, <a href="/wiki/Teke_people" title="Teke people">Teke</a>-Mbete, Mèmbè, <a href="/wiki/Kota_people_(Gabon)" title="Kota people (Gabon)">Kota</a>, Akélé.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> There are indigenous <a href="/wiki/Pygmy" class="mw-redirect" title="Pygmy">Pygmy</a> peoples: the <a href="/wiki/Bongo_people_(Gabon)" title="Bongo people (Gabon)">Bongo</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Baka_people_(Cameroon_and_Gabon)" title="Baka people (Cameroon and Gabon)">Baka</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-bn_12-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bn-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> The latter speak the only non-Bantu language in Gabon. More than 10,000 native French live in Gabon, including an estimated 2,000 dual nationals.<sup id="cite_ref-bn_12-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bn-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Some ethnicities are spread throughout Gabon, leading to contact, interaction among the groups, and intermarriage.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Population_centres">Population centres</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabon&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Population centres">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Languages">Languages</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabon&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Languages">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Languages_of_Gabon" title="Languages of Gabon">Languages of Gabon</a></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/French_language" title="French language">French</a> is the sole official language. It is estimated that 80% of the population can speak French, and that 30% of Libreville residents are native speakers of the language.
</p><p>Nationally, a majority of the Gabonese people speak indigenous languages, according to their ethnic group, while this proportion is lower than in most other Sub-Saharan African countries. The 2013 census found that 63.7% of Gabon's population could speak a Gabonese language, broken down by 86.3% in rural areas and 60.5% in urban areas speaking at least one national language.<sup id="cite_ref-recensement2013_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-recensement2013-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Religion">Religion</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabon&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Religion">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Religion_in_Gabon" title="Religion in Gabon">Religion in Gabon</a></div>
